}\left(k,j\right)\in A\text{ satisfy }\left\{\left\{i,k\right\},\left\{k,j\right\}\right\}\subseteq F\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{no }\left(i,k\right)\in A\text{ and
}\left(k,j\right)\in A\text{ satisfy }\left\{i,k\right\}\in F\text{
and }\left\{k,j\right\}\in F\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{no }\left(i,k\right)\in A\text{ and
}\left(k,j\right)\in A\text{ satisfy }\left(i,k\right)\in\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\text{ and }\left\{k,j\right\}\in F\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{because for }\left(i,k\right)\in A\text{, we have }\left\{i,k\right\}\in F\text{ if and only if }\left(i,k\right)\in\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.digraph.2pf-chrom.short.equiv}), applied to }u=i\text{
and }v=k\text{)}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{no }\left(i,k\right)\in A\text{ and
}\left(k,j\right)\in A\text{ satisfy }\left(i,k\right)\in\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\text{ and }\left(k,j\right)\in\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{because for }\left(k,j\right)\in A\text{, we have }\left\{k,j\right\}\in F\text{ if and only if }\left(k,j\right)\in\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.digraph.2pf-chrom.short.equiv}), applied to }u=k\text{
and }v=j\text{)}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{the digraph }\left(V,\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\right)\text{ is }2\text{-step-free}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of \textquotedblleft}2\text{-step-free\textquotedblright}\right),

qed.
. Now, Corollary
3.6
(applied to
X=\mathbb{N}
and

G=\underline{D}
) shows that

\displaystyle\chi_{\underline{D}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{
is }\mathfrak{K}\text{-free}\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
\text{the digraph }\left(V,\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\right)\text{ is
}2\text{-step-free}\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(since we have just shown that}\\
\text{a subset
}F\text{ of }E\text{ is }\mathfrak{K}\text{-free if and only if}\\
\text{the
digraph }\left(V,\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\right)\text{ is }2\text{-step-free)}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(-1\right)^{\left|\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\right|}\\
\text{(since }\pi\text{ is bijective)}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{x^{\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,F\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x^{\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{set}\left(\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\right)\right)}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.digraph.2pf-chrom.short.F=set}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
\text{the digraph }\left(V,\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\right)\text{ is }2\text{-step-free}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\right|}x^{\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{set}\left(\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\right)\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq A;\\
\text{the digraph }\left(V,B\right)\text{ is }2\text{-step-free}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}x^{\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{set}B\right)}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }B\text{ for }\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\text{
in the sum,}\\
\text{since the map }\pi:A\rightarrow E\text{ is bijective and thus induces}\\
\text{a bijection from the subsets of }E\text{ to the subsets of }A\\
\text{sending each }F\subseteq E\text{ to }\pi^{-1}\left(F\right)\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq A;\\
\text{the digraph }\left(V,F\right)\text{ is }2\text{-step-free}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}x^{\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{set}F\right)}

(here, we have renamed the summation index
B
as
F
). This proves
Proposition
4.5
.

5
Ambigraphs

5.1
Definitions of ambigraphs and proper colorings

We now move on to study various generalizations of the chromatic symmetric function.

The first generalization replaces the finite graph
G
by what we call an

ambigraph
(short for “ambiguous
graph”). To our knowledge, this is a new notion, but it
serves to unify two rather well-known concepts:

•

that of a
multigraph
(see
[
Grinbe21
, Definition 6.1.1]
),
which is like a graph but allows for multiple parallel edges
18
18

18

More
precisely, our notion of an ambigraph generalizes
loopless
multigraphs,
i.e., multigraphs with no loops. Loops would be a trivial but technically
awkward distraction in the study of chromatic polynomials, so we prefer to
leave them out of our notions of graphs.
;

•

that of a
hypergraph
(see
[
Berge73
, Chapter 17]
), which is
like a graph but allows its “edges” to have
any number of endpoints instead of two.

In both of these settings, chromatic polynomials have been defined long ago
(for multigraphs perhaps since the introduction of the
concept
19
19

19

Authors often leave it vague whether their graphs are simple
graphs or multigraphs.
; for hypergraphs since Dohmen’s
[
Dohmen95
]
), and
it is fairly straightforward to define chromatic symmetric functions at the
same levels of generality. However, we shall instead define them for

ambigraphs
, a concept which we now introduce:

Definition 5.1
.

(a)
An
ambigraph
shall mean a triple

\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
, where
V
and
E
are two sets, and where

\varphi:E\rightarrow\mathcal{P}\left(\dbinom{V}{2}\right)
is a map.
(Thus, the map
\varphi
sends each
e\in E
to a set of
2
-element subsets
of
V
.)

(b)
An ambigraph
\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
is said to be

finite
if
V
and
E
are finite.

(c)
Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an ambigraph. Then, the
elements of
V
are called the
vertices
of
G
, whereas the elements of

E
are called the
edgeries
of
G
. If
e\in E
is any edgery, then the
elements of
\varphi\left(e\right)
are called the
edges
of
e
.
Note that these edges are
2
-element subsets of
V
.

(d)
Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an ambigraph. An edgery

e\in E
is said to be
singleton
if it has exactly one edge (i.e., if

\left|\varphi\left(e\right)\right|=1
).

We view an ambigraph
\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
as something akin to a
graph, except that instead of having edges, it has edgeries – i.e., packages
of edges. (This can be equivalently viewed as an edge-colored graph, but we
eschew such an interpretation as we shall be using colors for other purposes.)

Example 5.2
.

Let
V
be the set
\left\{1,2,3,4,5\right\}
. Let

E
be the
6
-element set
\left\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4},e_{5},e_{6}\right\}
. Let
\varphi:E\rightarrow\mathcal{P}\left(\dbinom{V}{2}\right)
be the map defined as follows:

\displaystyle\varphi\left(e_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{2,5\right\}\right\},

\displaystyle\varphi\left(e_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left\{1,2\right\},\ \left\{2,3\right\},\ \left\{3,4\right\}\right\},

\displaystyle\varphi\left(e_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left\{2,5\right\}\right\},

\displaystyle\varphi\left(e_{4}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{2,5\right\}\right\},

\displaystyle\varphi\left(e_{5}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{{}\right\}=\varnothing,

\displaystyle\varphi\left(e_{6}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left\{2,3\right\},\ \left\{3,4\right\}\right\}.

Let
G
be the triple
\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
. Then,
G
is an
ambigraph. Its edgeries are
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4},e_{5},e_{6}
. The edgery

e_{3}
is singleton, while the other edgeries are not. The edgeries
e_{1}

and
e_{4}
contain the same edges, namely
\left\{1,3\right\}
and

\left\{2,5\right\}
.

Both multigraphs and hypergraphs can now be encoded as ambigraphs:

•

A multigraph can be viewed as an ambigraph whose all edgeries are
singleton
20
20

20

To be more precise, this is true for
loopless

multigraphs (i.e., multigraphs that have no loops). Loops can be encoded as
edgeries that have no edges.
.

•

A hypergraph can be encoded as an ambigraph by replacing each edge

\left\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\right\}
with an edgery consisting of all
edges
\left\{v_{i},v_{j}\right\}
with
i<j
. (Note that this encoding
turns
1
-element edges into empty edgeries
21
21

21

i.e., edgeries that have
no edges
. Empty edgeries trivialize most of our results, but do not
invalidate any of our proofs, so we have no reason to exclude them.)

We can now define
X
-colorings and proper
X
-colorings for ambigraphs:

Definition 5.3
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an ambigraph. Let
X
be a set.

(a)
An
X
-coloring
of
G
is defined to mean a map

V\rightarrow X
.

(b)
If
f:V\rightarrow X
is an
X
-coloring of
G
, and if
\left\{s,t\right\}
is a
2
-element subset of
V
, then this subset
\left\{s,t\right\}
is said to be
f
-dichromatic
if
f\left(s\right)\neq f\left(t\right)
.

(c)
An
X
-coloring
f
of
G
is said to be
proper
if each
edgery
e\in E
has at least one
f
-dichromatic edge (i.e., for each edgery

e\in E
, there exists at least one edge
\left\{s,t\right\}\in\varphi\left(e\right)
satisfying
f\left(s\right)\neq f\left(t\right)
).

Example 5.4
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be the
ambigraph from Example
5.2
. Then,
G
has no proper

X
-coloring for any
X
, since the edgery
e_{5}
will never have an

f
-dichromatic edge, no matter what
f
is (because
e_{5}
has no edge to
begin with).

However, let us now modify
\varphi
by replacing
\varphi\left(e_{5}\right)
by the set
\left\{\left\{1,4\right\},\ \left\{2,4\right\},\ \left\{3,4\right\}\right\}
. Then, for example, the

X
-coloring
f:V\rightarrow\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
given by

\displaystyle f\left(1\right)

\displaystyle=1,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(2\right)=2,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(3\right)=1,

\displaystyle f\left(4\right)

\displaystyle=1,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(5\right)=3,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(6\right)=1

is proper. For instance, the edgery
e_{1}
has the
f
-dichromatic edge

\left\{2,5\right\}
, whereas the edgery
e_{2}
has the two

f
-dichromatic edges
\left\{1,2\right\}
and
\left\{2,3\right\}
.
On the other hand, the
X
-coloring
f:V\rightarrow\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}

given by

\displaystyle f\left(1\right)

\displaystyle=1,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(2\right)=2,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(3\right)=2,

\displaystyle f\left(4\right)

\displaystyle=2,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(5\right)=3,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\left(6\right)=1

is not proper, since the edgery
e_{6}
has no
f
-dichromatic edge.

Example 5.5
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be the
ambigraph with
V=\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
and
E=\left\{a,b\right\}

and

\varphi\left(a\right)=\left\{\left\{2,3\right\}\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \varphi\left(b\right)=\left\{\left\{1,2\right\},\ \left\{3,4\right\}\right\}.

Let
X
be a set. Then, a map
f:V\rightarrow X
is a proper
X
-coloring of

G
if and only if it satisfies

f\left(2\right)\neq f\left(3\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(f\left(1\right)\neq f\left(2\right)\text{
or }f\left(3\right)\neq f\left(4\right)\right).

Indeed, the statement “
f\left(2\right)\neq f\left(3\right)
” is saying that the edgery
a
has an

f
-dichromatic edge, whereas the statement “
f\left(1\right)\neq f\left(2\right)
or
f\left(3\right)\neq f\left(4\right)
” is saying that the edgery
b
has an

f
-dichromatic edge.

As Example
5.5
illustrates, the condition on an

X
-coloring of
G
to be proper is a conjunction of disjunctions of
inequalities of the form
f\left(v\right)\neq f\left(w\right)
for

\left(v,w\right)\in V^{2}
.

Remark 5.6
.

Any graph
G=\left(V,E\right)
can be viewed as
an ambigraph
\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
in a fairly obvious way: viz., by
setting
\varphi\left(e\right)=\left\{e\right\}
for each edge
e\in E
. We shall denote the latter ambigraph by
G^{\operatorname*{amb}}
. The
proper
X
-colorings of this ambigraph
G^{\operatorname*{amb}}
are precisely
the proper
X
-colorings of the original graph
G
.

Remark 5.7
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an
ambigraph, and let
X
be a set. If there exists an edgery
e\in E
satisfying

\varphi\left(e\right)=\varnothing
, then there exists no proper

X
-coloring
f
of
G
(since the edgery
e
will never have an

f
-dichromatic edge).

5.2
The chromatic symmetric function of an ambigraph

We can now define the chromatic symmetric function of an ambigraph, by
imitating Definition
1.5
:

Definition 5.8
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite ambigraph.

(a)
For every
\mathbb{N}_{+}
-coloring
f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
of
G
, we let
\mathbf{x}_{f}
denote the monomial
\prod\limits_{v\in V}x_{f\left(v\right)}
in the indeterminates
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
.

(b)
We define a power series
X_{G}\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
by

X_{G}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ is a}\\
\text{proper
}\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring of }G\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}_{f}.

This power series
X_{G}
is called the
chromatic symmetric function
of

G
.

Example 5.9
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be the ambigraph from Example

5.5
. Then,

\displaystyle X_{G}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ is
a}\\
\text{proper }\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring of }G\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\mathbf{x}_{f}}_{=x_{f\left(1\right)}x_{f\left(2\right)}x_{f\left(3\right)}x_{f\left(4\right)}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ is a}\\
\text{proper
}\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring of }G\end{subarray}}x_{f\left(1\right)}x_{f\left(2\right)}x_{f\left(3\right)}x_{f\left(4\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+};\\
f\left(2\right)\neq f\left(3\right)\text{ and }\left(f\left(1\right)\neq f\left(2\right)\text{ or }f\left(3\right)\neq f\left(4\right)\right)\end{subarray}}x_{f\left(1\right)}x_{f\left(2\right)}x_{f\left(3\right)}x_{f\left(4\right)}

(since a map
f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
is a proper
\mathbb{N}_{+}
-coloring of
G
if and only if it satisfies
f\left(2\right)\neq f\left(3\right)
and
\left(f\left(1\right)\neq f\left(2\right)\text{ or }f\left(3\right)\neq f\left(4\right)\right)
). If we
re-encode each map
f:\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
as
the
4
-tuple
\left(i,j,k,\ell\right)=\left(f\left(1\right),f\left(2\right),f\left(3\right),f\left(4\right)\right)
of its
values, then we can rewrite this equality as

X_{G}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i,j,k,\ell\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{4};\\
j\neq k\text{ and }\left(i\neq j\text{ or }k\neq\ell\right)\end{subarray}}x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}x_{\ell}.

Remark 5.10
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an ambigraph that has an edgery
e\in E
satisfying
\varphi\left(e\right)=\varnothing
. Then, there exists no
proper
\mathbb{N}_{+}
-coloring
f
of
G
(by Remark

5.7
), and thus we have
X_{G}=0
.

5.3
The union of a set of edgeries

An ambigraph
\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
can be transformed into a simple
graph
\left(V,E^{\prime}\right)
by taking the union of some of its
edgeries – i.e., by setting
E^{\prime}:=\bigcup\limits_{e\in F}\varphi\left(e\right)
for some subset
F
of
E
. Let us give this construction a name:

Definition 5.11
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an
ambigraph. Let
F
be a subset of
E
. Then,
\operatorname*{union}F
shall
denote the subset
\bigcup\limits_{e\in F}\varphi\left(e\right)
of
\dbinom{V}{2}
. Thus, we obtain a graph
\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
.

Example 5.12
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be the ambigraph from Example

5.2
. Then,

\operatorname*{union}\left\{e_{2},e_{3}\right\}=\left\{\left\{1,2\right\},\ \left\{2,3\right\},\ \left\{3,4\right\},\ \left\{2,5\right\}\right\}

and

\operatorname*{union}\left\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{4}\right\}=\left\{\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{2,5\right\},\ \left\{1,2\right\},\ \left\{2,3\right\},\ \left\{3,4\right\}\right\}

and
\operatorname*{union}\left\{{}\right\}=\varnothing
.

We can use this notion to state our first result about ambigraphs – an
analogue to Theorem
1.8
. We shall prove this result at
the end of the next subsection.

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Then,

X_{G}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}p_{\lambda\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)}.

(Here, of course, the pair
\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is
regarded as a graph, and the expression
\lambda\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is understood according to Definition

1.7

(b)
.)

5.4
Circuits and broken circuits

Let us now define the notions of cycles, circuits and broken circuits of an ambigraph.

Definition 5.14
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an
ambigraph. A
cycle
of
G
denotes a list

\left(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},e_{2},\ldots,v_{m},e_{m},v_{m+1}\right)

with the following properties:

•

The entries
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{m+1}
at the odd positions of this
list belong to
V
, whereas the entries
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}
at its even
positions belong to
E
.

•

We have
m\geq 1
.

•

We have
v_{m+1}=v_{1}
.

•

The vertices
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{m}
are pairwise distinct.

•

The edgeries
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}
are pairwise distinct.

•

We have
\left\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\right\}\in\varphi\left(e_{i}\right)
for every
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,m\right\}
.

If
\left(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},e_{2},\ldots,v_{m},e_{m},v_{m+1}\right)
is a
cycle of
G
, then the set
\left\{e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}\right\}
is
called a
circuit
of
G
.

Example 5.15
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be the
ambigraph from Example
5.2
. Then, the tuple

\left(1,e_{2},2,e_{6},3,e_{4},1\right)

is a cycle of
G
(chiefly because
\left\{1,2\right\}\in\varphi\left(e_{2}\right)
and
\left\{2,3\right\}\in\varphi\left(e_{6}\right)

and
\left\{3,1\right\}\in\varphi\left(e_{4}\right)
). The circuit
corresponding to this cycle is
\left\{e_{2},e_{6},e_{4}\right\}
.

The tuple
\left(1,e_{2},2,e_{6},3,e_{1},1\right)
is a cycle of
G
as
well, and leads to the circuit
\left\{e_{2},e_{6},e_{1}\right\}
.

For comparison, the similar-looking tuple
\left(1,e_{2},2,e_{2},3,e_{4},1\right)
is not a cycle, since its edgeries
e_{2},e_{2},e_{4}

are not distinct.

Definition 5.16
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an ambigraph.
Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a function. We
shall refer to
\ell
as the
labeling function
. For every edgery
e
of

G
, we shall refer to
\ell\left(e\right)
as the
label
of
e
.

A
broken circuit
of
G
means a subset of
E
having the form

C\setminus\left\{e\right\}
, where
C
is a circuit of
G
, and where
e

is the unique singleton edgery in
C
having maximum label (among the
singleton edgeries in
C
). Of course, the notion of a broken circuit of
G

depends on the function
\ell
; however, we suppress the mention of
\ell
in
our notation, since we will not consider situations where two different
\ell
’s coexist.

Thus, if
G
is an ambigraph with a labeling function
\ell
, then any circuit

C
of
G
gives rise to a broken circuit provided that

•

at least one edgery in
C
is singleton, and

•

among the singleton edgeries in
C
, only one attains the maximum label.

In all other cases,
C
does not give rise to a broken circuit.
Notice that two different circuits may give rise to one and the same broken circuit.

Example 5.17
.

(a)
Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be the ambigraph from
Example
5.2
. Let
X
and
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be arbitrary.
Then, the circuit
\left\{e_{2},e_{6},e_{4}\right\}
we found in Example

5.15
does not give rise to a broken circuit, since it
contains no singleton edgery. However, the circuit
\left\{e_{3},e_{1}\right\}
(coming from the cycle
\left(2,e_{3},5,e_{1},2\right)

does give rise to a broken circuit (namely,
\left\{e_{1}\right\}
), since
its unique singleton edgery is
e_{3}
.

(b)
For better examples, we can try an ambigraph having more
singleton edgeries. For instance, we can choose some graph
G
and consider
the corresponding ambigraph
G^{\operatorname*{amb}}
as defined in Remark

5.6
. Then, the broken circuits of
G^{\operatorname*{amb}}
are precisely the broken circuits of
G
.

(c)
Here is another example: Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be
the ambigraph with
V=\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
,
E=\left\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\right\}
and

\varphi\left(e_{1}\right)=\left\{\left\{1,2\right\}\right\},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \varphi\left(e_{2}\right)=\left\{\left\{2,3\right\}\right\},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \varphi\left(e_{3}\right)=\left\{\left\{3,4\right\},\ \left\{1,3\right\}\right\}.

Let
X
and
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be arbitrary. Then, the cycle
\left(1,e_{1},2,e_{2},3,e_{3},1\right)
of
G
gives rise to the circuit
\left\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\right\}
. This circuit gives rise to

•

the broken circuit
\left\{e_{2},e_{3}\right\}
if
\ell\left(e_{1}\right)>\ell\left(e_{2}\right)
;

•

the broken circuit
\left\{e_{1},e_{3}\right\}
if
\ell\left(e_{1}\right)<\ell\left(e_{2}\right)
;

•

no broken circuit if
\ell\left(e_{1}\right)=\ell\left(e_{2}\right)
.

Note that
\ell\left(e_{3}\right)
does not matter, since the
edgery
e_{3}
is not singleton.

The notion of a broken circuit always depends on a labeling function

\ell:E\rightarrow X
. Any time we speak about broken circuits, we shall
tacitly understand that the function
\ell:E\rightarrow X
is used as the
labeling function.

5.5
The main results for ambigraphs

We can now generalize Theorem
1.12
to ambigraphs:

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a
labeling function. Let
\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of
G

(not necessarily containing all of them). Let
a_{K}
be an element of

\mathbf{k}
for every
K\in\mathfrak{K}
. Then,

X_{G}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq F\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)p_{\lambda\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)}.

(Here, of course, the pair
\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is
regarded as a graph, and the expression
\lambda\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is understood according to Definition

1.7

(b)
.)

This theorem generalizes Theorem
1.12
(in fact, the latter
is easily obtained by applying the former to
G^{\operatorname*{amb}}
instead
of
G
). Before we prove it, let us first explore some particular cases. Using
Definition
1.13
, we can obtain the following consequences of
Theorem
1.12
:

Corollary 5.19
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a
finite ambigraph. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X

be a labeling function. Let
\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of

G
(not necessarily containing all of them). Then,

X_{G}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{ is }\mathfrak{K}\text{-free}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}p_{\lambda\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)}.

Corollary 5.20
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a
labeling function. Then,

X_{G}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{ contains no broken}\\
\text{circuit of }G\text{ as a subset}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}p_{\lambda\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)}.

5.6
Proofs

Our proof of Theorem
5.18
is mostly similar to our
above proof of Theorem
1.12
, but there are some
complications due to the possibility of non-singleton edgeries.

We shall use the Iverson bracket notation (Definition
2.11
). We
begin with a basic cancellation lemma (see, e.g.,
[
Grinbe20
, Proposition
7.8.10]
):

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
S
be a finite set. Then,
\sum\limits_{I\subseteq S}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\right|}=\left[S=\varnothing\right]
.

In Definition
2.1
, we defined a set
\operatorname*{Eqs}f
for any
map
f:V\rightarrow X
. This set helped us find the edges of a graph whose
endpoints received the same color under a coloring
f
. We shall now introduce
a similar notion for ambigraphs:

Definition 5.22
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an ambigraph. Let

X
be a set. Let
f:V\rightarrow X
be a map. We let
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
denote the subset

\left\{e\in E\ \mid\ \varphi\left(e\right)\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\right\}

of
E
.

Example 5.23
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be the ambigraph with
V=\left\{1,2,3,4,5,6\right\}
and
E=\left\{a,b,c\right\}
and

\displaystyle\varphi\left(a\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{2,4\right\},\ \left\{3,6\right\}\right\},

\displaystyle\varphi\left(b\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{2,6\right\}\right\},

\displaystyle\varphi\left(c\right)

\displaystyle=\varnothing.

Let
X=\mathbb{N}
, and let
f:V\rightarrow X
be the map that sends

1,2,3,4,5,6
to
1,2,1,2,1,2
, respectively. Then,

\operatorname*{Eqs}f=\left\{\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{1,5\right\},\ \left\{3,5\right\},\ \left\{2,4\right\},\ \left\{2,6\right\},\ \left\{4,6\right\}\right\}

and
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)=\left\{b,c\right\}
. Indeed,
we have
b\in\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
since
\varphi\left(b\right)=\left\{\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{2,6\right\}\right\}\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f
, and we have
c\in\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
since
\varphi\left(c\right)=\varnothing\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f
. On the other hand,
a\notin\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
since
\varphi\left(a\right)\not\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f
(because
\left\{3,6\right\}
belongs to

\varphi\left(a\right)
but not to
\operatorname*{Eqs}f
).

Remark 5.24
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an
ambigraph. Let
X
be a set. Let
f:V\rightarrow X
be a map. The definition
of
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
yields

\displaystyle\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{e\in E\ \mid\ \varphi\left(e\right)\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\right\}

(19)

\displaystyle=\left\{d\in E\ \mid\ \varphi\left(d\right)\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\right\}

(20)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have renamed
the index }e\text{ as }d\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{d\in E\ \mid\ \text{no edge of }d\text{ is }f\text{-dichromatic}\right\}.

(21)

(The last equality is easy to check from the definitions.)

In analogy to Lemma
2.3
, we can use
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
to characterize when an
X
-coloring
f
is proper:

Lemma 5.25
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an
ambigraph. Let
X
be a set. Let
f:V\rightarrow X
be a map. Then, the

X
-coloring
f
of
G
is proper if and only if
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)=\varnothing
.

Proof 5.26

(Proof of Lemma
5.25
.)
.

An exercise in unfolding definitions
and applying de Morgan’s laws.

The following simple lemma connects
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)

with the
\operatorname*{union}F
construction from Definition

5.11
:

Lemma 5.27
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an ambigraph.
Let
X
be a set. Let
f:V\rightarrow X
be a map. Let
B
be a subset of
E
.
Then,
B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
holds if and only if

\operatorname*{union}B\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f
.

Proof 5.28

(Proof of Lemma
5.27
.)
.

The definition of

\operatorname*{union}B
yields
\operatorname*{union}B=\bigcup\limits_{e\in B}\varphi\left(e\right)
. Hence, we have the following chain of logical
equivalences:

\displaystyle\ \left(\operatorname*{union}B\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\bigcup\limits_{e\in B}\varphi\left(e\right)\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\varphi\left(e\right)\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\text{ for each }e\in B\right).

(22)

However, for an edgery
e\in E
, the condition
\varphi\left(e\right)\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f
is equivalent to
e\in\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
(by the definition of
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
). Hence, we can rewrite the equivalence
(
22
) as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\operatorname*{union}B\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\right)\

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(e\in\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\text{
for each }e\in B\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\right).

In other words,
B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
holds if
and only if
\operatorname*{union}B\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f
. This proves
Lemma
5.27
.

Next, let us show an analogue of Lemma
2.5
:

Lemma 5.29
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an
ambigraph. Let
X
be a set. Let
f:V\rightarrow X
be a map. Let
C
be a
circuit of
G
. Let
e\in C
be a singleton edgery such that
C\setminus\left\{e\right\}\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
. Then,

e\in\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
.

Proof 5.30

(Proof of Lemma
5.29
.)
.

We have assumed that
e
is
singleton. In other words,
e
has exactly one edge. In other words,

\varphi\left(e\right)
is a
1
-element set.

The set
C
is a circuit of
G
. Hence, we can write
C
in the form

C=\left\{e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}\right\}

for some cycle
\left(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},e_{2},\ldots,v_{m},e_{m},v_{m+1}\right)
of
G
. Consider this cycle

\left(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},e_{2},\ldots,v_{m},e_{m},v_{m+1}\right)
. According to the
definition of a “cycle”, the cycle
\left(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},e_{2},\ldots,v_{m},e_{m},v_{m+1}\right)
is a list having
the following properties:

•

The entries
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{m+1}
at the odd positions of this
list belong to
V
, whereas the entries
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}
at its even
positions belong to
E
.

•

We have
m\geq 1
.

•

We have
v_{m+1}=v_{1}
.

•

The vertices
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{m}
are pairwise distinct.

•

The edgeries
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}
are pairwise distinct.

•

We have
\left\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\right\}\in\varphi\left(e_{i}\right)
for every
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,m\right\}
.

Recall that
e\in C=\left\{e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}\right\}
. We can thus
WLOG assume that
e=e_{m}
(since otherwise, we can simply cyclically relabel
the vertices and the edgeries along our cycle). Assume this. Since
C=\left\{e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}\right\}
and
e=e_{m}
, we have

C\setminus\left\{e\right\}=\left\{e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m-1}\right\}

(since the
m
edgeries
e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m}
are distinct). For every

i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,m-1\right\}
, we thus have
e_{i}\in C\setminus\left\{e\right\}\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
and
therefore
\varphi\left(e_{i}\right)\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f
(by the
definition of
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
), so that
\left\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\right\}\in\varphi\left(e_{i}\right)\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f
and therefore
f\left(v_{i}\right)=f\left(v_{i+1}\right)
(by the definition of
\operatorname*{Eqs}f
). Hence,

f\left(v_{1}\right)=f\left(v_{2}\right)=\cdots=f\left(v_{m}\right)
, so that
f\left(v_{m}\right)=f\left(v_{1}\right)
. Thus,
\left\{v_{m},v_{1}\right\}\in\operatorname*{Eqs}f
.

However, recall again that
\left\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\right\}\in\varphi\left(e_{i}\right)
for every
i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,m\right\}
. Applying this
to
i=m
, we obtain
\left\{v_{m},v_{m+1}\right\}\in\varphi\left(e_{m}\right)
. Since
v_{m+1}=v_{1}
and
e_{m}=e
, we can rewrite this as

\left\{v_{m},v_{1}\right\}\in\varphi\left(e\right)
. Since

\varphi\left(e\right)
is a
1
-element set, this entails that

\varphi\left(e\right)=\left\{\left\{v_{m},v_{1}\right\}\right\}\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f
(since
\left\{v_{m},v_{1}\right\}\in\operatorname*{Eqs}f
). In other words,
e\in\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
(by the definition of
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
). This proves Lemma
5.29
.

Our next lemma will play a role in our proof of Theorem

5.18
that is similar to the role of Lemma

2.9
in the proof of Theorem
1.12

(although it is different in its claim).

Lemma 5.31
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be an ambigraph.
Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a labeling function.

Let
Y
be any set. Let
f:V\rightarrow Y
be any map. Assume that the set

\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
contains no singleton edgery. Then,
there exists no broken circuit
K
of
G
satisfying
K\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
.

Proof 5.32

(Proof of Lemma
5.31
.)
.

Assume the contrary. Thus, there
exists some broken circuit
K
of
G
satisfying
K\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
.

The set
K
is a broken circuit of
G
. According to the definition of a
broken circuit, this means that
K
can be written as
K=C\setminus\left\{e\right\}
, where
C
is a circuit of
G
, and where
e
is the unique
singleton edgery in
C
having maximum label (among the singleton edgeries in

C
). Consider these
C
and
e
.

We have
C\setminus\left\{e\right\}=K\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
. Thus, Lemma
5.29
(applied to
Y
instead
of
X
) yields that
e\in\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
. Thus, the
set
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
contains a singleton edgery
(namely,
e
). But this contradicts the fact that the set
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
contains no singleton edgery.

This contradiction shows that our assumption was false. Hence, Lemma

5.31
is proven.

We are now ready to prove the keystone lemma, which of course is an analogue
of Lemma
2.12
:

Lemma 5.33
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a
labeling function. Let
\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of
G

(not necessarily containing all of them). Let
a_{K}
be an element of

\mathbf{k}
for every
K\in\mathfrak{K}
.

Let
Y
be any set. Let
f:V\rightarrow Y
be any map. Then,

\sum\limits_{B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}=\left[\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)=\varnothing\right].

Proof 5.34

(Proof of Lemma
5.33
.)
.

We are in one of the following two cases:

Case 1:
The set
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
contains no
singleton edgery.

Case 2:
The set
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
contains at
least one singleton edgery.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case, the set
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
contains no singleton edgery. Hence, using Lemma

5.31
, it is easy to see that every subset
B
of

\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
satisfies

\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}=1.

(23)

Proof of (
23
):
Let
B
be a subset of

\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
. Then, Lemma
5.31

yields that there exists no broken circuit
K
of
G
satisfying

K\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
. Hence, there exists no

K\in\mathfrak{K}
satisfying
K\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
(since each
K\in\mathfrak{K}
is a broken circuit of
G
).
Therefore, there exists no
K\in\mathfrak{K}
satisfying
K\subseteq B
either
(since
K\subseteq B
would entail
K\subseteq B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
, which would contradict the previous sentence). Hence,
the product
\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}
is
empty, and thus equals
1
by definition. This proves
(
23
).

Now,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\underbrace{\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.lem.ambiNBCm.moeb.c1.1}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}=\sum\limits_{I\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)}\left(-1\right)^{\left|I\right|}

\displaystyle=\left[\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)=\varnothing\right]

(by Lemma
5.21
, applied to
S=\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
). Thus, Lemma
5.33
is proved in Case 1.

Let us now consider Case 2. In this case, the set
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
contains at least one singleton edgery. Pick any such singleton
edgery
d\in\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
with maximum

d\in\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
(among all such singleton edgeries).

Define two subsets
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{P}\left(\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\right)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle=\left\{F\in\mathcal{P}\left(\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\right)\ \mid\ d\notin F\right\};

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle=\left\{F\in\mathcal{P}\left(\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\right)\ \mid\ d\in F\right\}.

Thus, we have
\mathcal{P}\left(\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\right)=\mathcal{U}\cup\mathcal{V}
, and the sets
\mathcal{U}
and

\mathcal{V}
are disjoint. Now, we define a map
\Phi:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
by

\left(\Phi\left(B\right)=B\cup\left\{d\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for every }B\in\mathcal{U}\right).

As in our above proof of Lemma
2.12
, we can see that this map

\Phi
is well-defined and a bijection, and that every
B\in\mathcal{U}

satisfies

\left(-1\right)^{\left|\Phi\left(B\right)\right|}=-\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}.

(24)

Furthermore, we claim that, for every
B\in\mathcal{U}
and every

K\in\mathfrak{K}
, we have the following logical equivalence:

\left(K\subseteq B\right)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \left(K\subseteq\Phi\left(B\right)\right).

(25)

Indeed, our above proof of (
10
) can be
easily transformed into a proof of (
25
) by some simple changes
22
22

22

Here are the changes that we need to make to
the above proof: We need to replace “edge” by “singleton edgery”; replace both sets

E\cap\operatorname*{Eqs}f
and
\operatorname*{Eqs}f
by
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
; and replace the reference to Lemma

2.5
by a reference to Lemma
5.29
.
.
Thus, (
25
) holds.

Now, just as in the proof of Lemma
2.12
, we can conclude that

\sum\limits_{B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}=0.

(26)

However, the set
\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)
contains at least
one singleton edgery, and thus is nonempty. Hence,
\left[\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)=\varnothing\right]=0
. Comparing
this with (
26
), we obtain

\sum\limits_{B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}=\left[\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)=\varnothing\right]
. Thus, Lemma
5.33
is proved in Case 2.

We have now proved Lemma
5.33
in both Cases 1 and 2. Since
these two cases cover all possibilities, we thus have proved Lemma

5.33
.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
5.18
and Corollaries

5.19
and
5.20
as well as
Theorem
5.13
:

Proof 5.35

(Proof of Theorem
5.18
.)
.

The definition of
X_{G}

shows that

\displaystyle X_{G}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ is
a}\\
\text{proper }\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring of }G\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}_{f}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}}\left[\underbrace{f\text{ is a proper
}\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring of }G}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\text{the }\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring }f\text{ of }G\text{ is
proper}\right)\\
\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)=\varnothing\right)\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.ambiEqs.proper},
applied to }\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ instead of }X\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right]\mathbf{x}_{f}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}}\underbrace{\left[\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)=\varnothing\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\\
\text{(by Lemma \ref{lem.ambiNBCm.moeb}, applied to }Y=\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}_{f}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(since }\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\text{ is a subset of
}E\text{)}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)\mathbf{x}_{f}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{B\subseteq E}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+};\\
B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\end{subarray}}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)\mathbf{x}_{f}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{B\subseteq E}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+};\\
B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)\mathbf{x}_{f}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{B\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+};\\
B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+};\\
\operatorname*{union}B\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(since Lemma \ref{lem.ambiEqs.union}}\\
\text{(applied to }X=\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{) yields that}\\
\text{the
condition }B\subseteq\operatorname*{EQS}\left(G,f\right)\text{
(on}\\
\text{a map }f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{) is}\\
\text{equivalent
to }\operatorname*{union}B\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\text{)}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}_{f}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{B\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+};\\
\operatorname*{union}B\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}_{f}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=p_{\lambda\left(V,\operatorname*{union}B\right)}\\
\text{(by
Lemma \ref{lem.Eqs.sum}}\\
\text{(applied to }\operatorname*{union}B\text{
instead of }B\text{))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{B\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)p_{\lambda\left(V,\operatorname*{union}B\right)}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq F\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)p_{\lambda\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)}

(here, we have renamed the summation index
B
as
F
). This proves Theorem

5.18
.

Proof 5.36

(Proof of Corollary
5.19
.)
.

Analogous to the proof of
Corollary
1.14
.

Proof 5.37

(Proof of Corollary
5.20
.)
.

Corollary

5.20
follows from Corollary

5.19
when
\mathfrak{K}
is set to be the set of

all
broken circuits of
G
.

Proof 5.38

(Proof of Theorem
5.13
.)
.

This follows from Theorem

5.18
in the same way as Theorem

1.8
follows from Theorem
1.12
.

5.7
The chromatic polynomial

We have thus proved analogues of Theorems
1.8
and

1.12
and Corollaries
1.14
and

1.15
for ambigraphs. We can just as easily prove analogues
of Theorem
3.1
, Definition
3.2
, Theorems

3.4
and
3.5
and Corollaries

3.6
and
3.7
. Here they are, in the
order in which we have just mentioned them:

Theorem 5.39
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Then, there exists a unique polynomial
P\in\mathbb{Z}\left[x\right]
such that every
q\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

P\left(q\right)=\left(\text{the number of all proper }\left\{1,2,\ldots,q\right\}\text{-colorings of }G\right).

Definition 5.40
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Theorem
5.39
shows that there exists a
polynomial
P\in\mathbb{Z}\left[x\right]
such that every
q\in\mathbb{N}

satisfies
P\left(q\right)=\left(\text{the number of all proper
}\left\{1,2,\ldots,q\right\}\text{-colorings of }G\right)
. This
polynomial
P
is called the
chromatic polynomial
of
G
, and will be
denoted by
\chi_{G}
.

Theorem 5.41
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Then,

\chi_{G}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}x^{\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)}.

(Here, of course, the pair
\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is
regarded as a graph, and the expression
\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is understood according to Definition

3.3
.)

Theorem 5.42
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a
labeling function. Let
\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of
G

(not necessarily containing all of them). Let
a_{K}
be an element of

\mathbf{k}
for every
K\in\mathfrak{K}
. Then,

\chi_{G}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq F\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)x^{\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)}.

(Here, of course, the pair
\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is
regarded as a graph, and the expression
\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is understood according to Definition

3.3
.)

Corollary 5.43
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a
finite ambigraph. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X

be a labeling function. Let
\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of

G
(not necessarily containing all of them). Then,

\chi_{G}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{ is }\mathfrak{K}\text{-free}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}x^{\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)}.

Corollary 5.44
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a
labeling function. Then,

\chi_{G}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{ contains no broken}\\
\text{circuit of }G\text{ as a subset}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}x^{\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)}.

The proofs of all these results are analogous to the proofs of the
corresponding results from Section
3
, so we leave them all to
the reader.

One may reasonably wonder whether Corollary
3.21
has an
analogue for ambigraphs as well, i.e., whether one can replace the exponent

\operatorname*{conn}\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
in Corollary

5.44
by something simpler when
\ell
is injective. In
the case of a hypergraph, Dohmen has obtained such a result (
[
Dohmen95
, Theorem
2.1]
) under the additional condition that each cycle of
G
have at
least one singleton edgery. Unfortunately, for ambigraphs, such a
simplification does not appear possible (even under a condition like Dohmen’s).

6
Weighted and noncommutative versions

In the recent decades, the chromatic symmetric function of a graph has been
generalized in several directions. Two of them are the
chromatic
symmetric function of a weighted graph
as defined by Crew and Spirkl
(
[
CreSpi19
, §3]
), and the
noncommutative chromatic symmetric
function
of Gebhard and Sagan (
[
GebSag01
, §3]
). In this section, we
will recall the definitions of both of these generalizations, and extend our
results to them. (The extensions will be fairly mechanical, as all the hard
work has already been done.)

6.1
Weighted graphs and their chromatic symmetric functions

For us, a
weighted graph
will just mean a pair consisting of a graph

G=\left(V,E\right)
and a weight function on
V
. Weight functions are
defined as follows:

Definition 6.1
.

Let
V
be a set. A
weight function
on
V
means a
function
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
. If
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
is
a weight function on
V
, then the
weight
of an element
v\in V
is
defined to be the positive integer
w\left(v\right)\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.

Thus, a weight function on a set
V
just assigns a “weight” (a positive integer) to each element of
V
. Given
such a weight function for a graph
G=\left(V,E\right)
, we can define a
“weighted chromatic symmetric function”:

Definition 6.2
.

Let
G=\left(V,E\right)
be a finite graph. Let

w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on
V
.

(a)
For every
\mathbb{N}_{+}
-coloring
f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
of
G
, we let
\mathbf{x}_{f,w}
denote the monomial
\prod\limits_{v\in V}x_{f\left(v\right)}^{w\left(v\right)}
in the indeterminates

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
.

(b)
We define a power series
X_{G,w}\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
by

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ is a}\\
\text{proper
}\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring of }G\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}_{f,w}.

This power series
X_{G,w}
is called the
chromatic symmetric function

of
\left(G,w\right)
.

This chromatic symmetric function
X_{G,w}
was introduced by Crew and Spirkl
in
[
CreSpi19
, (1)]
(where it was denoted
X_{\left(G,w\right)}
). It
generalizes the original chromatic symmetric function
X_{G}
, which is
obtained when all the weights
w\left(v\right)
are
1
:

Example 6.3
.

Let
G=\left(V,E\right)
be a finite graph. Let

w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be the weight function that sends each
v\in V

to
1
. Then, for every
\mathbb{N}_{+}
-coloring
f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
of
G
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{f,w}

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{v\in V}\underbrace{x_{f\left(v\right)}^{w\left(v\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x_{f\left(v\right)}\\
\text{(since
}w\left(v\right)=1\\
\text{(by the definition of }w\text{))}\end{subarray}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by Definition \ref{def.wchromsym}
{(a)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{v\in V}x_{f\left(v\right)}=\mathbf{x}_{f}

(27)

(see Definition
1.5

(a)
for the definition of

\mathbf{x}_{f}
). Thus, Definition
6.2

(b)
yields

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ is a}\\
\text{proper
}\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring of }G\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\mathbf{x}_{f,w}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mathbf{x}_{f}\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.exa.wchromsym.w=1.1}))}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ is a}\\
\text{proper
}\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring of }G\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}_{f}=X_{G}

(by Definition
1.5

(b)
).

6.2
The weight of a subset and the partition
\lambda\left(G,w\right)

To state Whitney-like formulas for
X_{G,w}
, we need to adapt the partition

\lambda\left(G\right)
defined in Definition
1.7

(b)
to the case of a weighted graph. This adaptation consists in
replacing the size of each connected component by its
weight
. Here, the
weight of a subset of
V
is defined as follows:

Definition 6.4
.

Let
V
be a finite set. Let
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on
V
. Let
S
be a subset of
V
. Then,
the
weight
of
S
means the nonnegative integer
\sum\limits_{v\in S}w\left(v\right)
. This weight is denoted by
w\left(S\right)
.

Note that if the subset
S
in this definition is nonempty, then its weight

w\left(S\right)
is a positive integer, since it is defined as the
nonempty sum
\sum\limits_{v\in S}w\left(v\right)
of the positive weights

w\left(v\right)
.

For example,
w\left(\left\{2,5,6\right\}\right)=w\left(2\right)+w\left(5\right)+w\left(6\right)
(if
\left\{2,5,6\right\}
is a
subset of
V
).

Now, we can define the analogue of the partition
\lambda\left(G\right)

for a weighted graph:

Definition 6.5
.

Let
G=\left(V,E\right)
be a finite graph. Let

w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on
V
. We let

\lambda\left(G,w\right)
denote the list of the weights of all connected
components of
G
, in weakly decreasing order. (Each connected component
should contribute only one entry to the list.) We view
\lambda\left(G,w\right)
as a partition (since
\lambda\left(G,w\right)
is a weakly
decreasing finite list of positive integers).

Example 6.6
.

Let
G=\left(V,E\right)
be the finite graph with
V=\left\{1,2,3,4,5\right\}
and
E=\left\{\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{2,4\right\},\ \left\{2,5\right\}\right\}
. Then, the connected
components of
G
are
A=\left\{1,3\right\}
and
B=\left\{2,4,5\right\}
.

Let
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be the weight function given by
w\left(1\right)=6
and
w\left(2\right)=9
and
w\left(3\right)=6
and

w\left(4\right)=1
and
w\left(5\right)=3
. Then, the weights of the
connected components
A
and
B
are

\displaystyle w\left(A\right)

\displaystyle=w\left(\left\{1,3\right\}\right)=w\left(1\right)+w\left(3\right)=6+6=12\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle w\left(B\right)

\displaystyle=w\left(\left\{2,4,5\right\}\right)=w\left(2\right)+w\left(4\right)+w\left(5\right)=9+1+3=13.

Hence, the partition
\lambda\left(G,w\right)
is the list of these two
weights
12
and
13
, in weakly decreasing order. In other words,

\lambda\left(G,w\right)=\left(13,12\right)
.

6.3
Formulas for
X_{G,w}

We are now ready to state analogues of Theorems
1.8
and

1.12
and Corollaries
1.14
and

1.15
for weighted graphs:

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
G=\left(V,E\right)
be a finite graph. Let

w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on
V
. Then,

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}p_{\lambda\left(\left(V,F\right),w\right)}.

(Here, of course, the pair
\left(V,F\right)
is regarded as a graph, and
the expression
\lambda\left(\left(V,F\right),w\right)
is understood
according to Definition
6.5
.)

Theorem 6.8
.

Let
G=\left(V,E\right)
be a finite graph. Let

w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on
V
. Let
X
be a
totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a labeling function. Let

\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of
G
(not necessarily
containing all of them). Let
a_{K}
be an element of
\mathbf{k}
for every

K\in\mathfrak{K}
. Then,

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq F\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)p_{\lambda\left(\left(V,F\right),w\right)}.

(Here, of course, the pair
\left(V,F\right)
is regarded as a graph, and
the expression
\lambda\left(\left(V,F\right),w\right)
is understood
according to Definition
6.5
.)

Corollary 6.9
.

Let
G=\left(V,E\right)
be a finite graph.
Let
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on
V
. Let
X
be a
totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a labeling function. Let

\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of
G
(not necessarily
containing all of them). Then,

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{ is }\mathfrak{K}\text{-free}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}p_{\lambda\left(\left(V,F\right),w\right)}.

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
G=\left(V,E\right)
be a finite graph. Let

w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on
V
. Let
X
be a
totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a labeling function. Then,

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{ contains no broken}\\
\text{circuit of }G\text{ as a subset}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}p_{\lambda\left(\left(V,F\right),w\right)}.

Note that Theorem
6.7
is a result by Crew and Spirkl
(namely,
[
CreSpi19
, Lemma 3]
).

6.4
Proofs

In this section, we shall prove Theorem
6.8
, Corollary

6.9
, Corollary
6.10
and Theorem

6.7
. This will be fairly easy, as many of our above
lemmas can be reused without any change. However, we need the following
weighted version of Lemma
2.7
:

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
\left(V,B\right)
be a finite graph. Let

w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on
V
. Then,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+};\\
B\subseteq\operatorname*{Eqs}f\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}_{f,w}=p_{\lambda\left(\left(V,B\right),w\right)}.

(Here,
\mathbf{x}_{f,w}
is defined as in Definition
6.2

(a)
, and the expression
\lambda\left(\left(V,B\right),w\right)
is understood according to Definition
6.5
.)

Proof 6.12

(Proof of Lemma
6.11
.)
.

This is almost completely analogous to the
proof of Lemma
2.7
that we gave long ago. (Replace each size

\left|C_{i}\right|
by the weight
w\left(C_{i}\right)
;
replace all monomials
\mathbf{x}_{g}
by their weighted analogues

\mathbf{x}_{g,w}
; and of course, replace
\lambda\left(V,B\right)
by

\lambda\left(\left(V,B\right),w\right)
.)

It is now straightforward to adapt our above proofs of Theorem

1.12
, Corollary
1.14
, Corollary

1.15
and Theorem
1.8
to obtain proofs
of Theorem
6.8
, Corollary
6.9
,
Corollary
6.10
and Theorem
6.7
. Of
course, Lemma
6.11
needs to be used instead of Lemma

2.7
, but everything else stays almost completely unchanged. We
leave the details to the reader.

6.5
Ambigraphs redux

Just as we have imposed weights on the vertices of a graph, we can do the same
to the vertices of an ambigraph. This leads to a generalization of the
chromatic symmetric function
X_{G}
we introduced in Definition

5.8
:

Definition 6.13
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Let
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on
V
.

(a)
For every
\mathbb{N}_{+}
-coloring
f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
of
G
, we let
\mathbf{x}_{f,w}
denote the monomial
\prod\limits_{v\in V}x_{f\left(v\right)}^{w\left(v\right)}
in the indeterminates

x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
.

(b)
We define a power series
X_{G,w}\in\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
by

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ is a}\\
\text{proper
}\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring of }G\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}_{f,w}.

This power series
X_{G,w}
is called the
chromatic symmetric function

of
\left(G,w\right)
.

We can now state generalizations of Theorems
5.13
and

5.18
and Corollaries
5.19
and

5.20
:

Theorem 6.14
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a
finite ambigraph. Let
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on

V
. Then,

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}p_{\lambda\left(\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right),w\right)}.

(Here, of course, the pair
\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is
regarded as a graph, and the expression
\lambda\left(\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right),w\right)
is understood according to
Definition
6.5
.)

Theorem 6.15
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a
finite ambigraph. Let
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on

V
. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a labeling
function. Let
\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of
G
(not
necessarily containing all of them). Let
a_{K}
be an element of
\mathbf{k}

for every
K\in\mathfrak{K}
. Then,

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq F\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)p_{\lambda\left(\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right),w\right)}.

(Here, of course, the pair
\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is
regarded as a graph, and the expression
\lambda\left(\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right),w\right)
is understood according to
Definition
6.5
.)

Corollary 6.16
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a
finite ambigraph. Let
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on

V
. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a labeling
function. Let
\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of
G
(not
necessarily containing all of them). Then,

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{ is }\mathfrak{K}\text{-free}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}p_{\lambda\left(\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right),w\right)}.

Corollary 6.17
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Let
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be a weight function on
V
. Let

X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a labeling
function. Then,

X_{G,w}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{ contains no broken}\\
\text{circuit of }G\text{ as a subset}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}p_{\lambda\left(\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right),w\right)}.

The proofs of these four results proceed precisely like their non-weighted
counterparts, again using Lemma
6.11
instead of Lemma

2.7
.

6.6
Noncommutative chromatic symmetric functions

The
noncommutative chromatic symmetric function

Y_{G}
of a graph
G

has been introduced by Gebhard and Sagan in
[
GebSag01
, §3]
as a lift of
the chromatic symmetric function
X_{G}
to a noncommutative polynomial ring.
In order to define it, we need to lift the monomials
\mathbf{x}_{f}
to
noncommutative monomials, which requires fixing a list of the vertices of
G

(since a noncommutative product is only defined if its factors appear in a
chosen order). Unlike
[
GebSag01
]
, we shall not require this list to
contain each vertex of
G
exactly once; thus, we obtain a more general notion
that refines not only Stanley’s original
X_{G}
but also its weighted version

X_{G,w}
discussed above.

We consider the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
\mathbf{k}\left\langle\left\langle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots\right\rangle\right\rangle
of noncommutative power
series in countably many distinct indeterminates
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots

over
\mathbf{k}
. It is a topological
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
23
23

23

Its
topology is defined in the same way as the topology on
\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
(but of course the
monomials are now noncommutative monomials).
. A noncommutative power series

P\in\mathbf{k}\left\langle\left\langle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots\right\rangle\right\rangle
is said to be
bounded-degree
if there exists an

N\in\mathbb{N}
such that every noncommutative monomial of degree
>N

appears with coefficient
0
in
P
. A noncommutative power series

P\in\mathbf{k}\left\langle\left\langle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots\right\rangle\right\rangle
is said to be
symmetric
if and only if
P
is invariant
under any permutation of the indeterminates. We let
\Lambda_{\operatorname*{NC}}
be the subset of
\mathbf{k}\left\langle\left\langle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots\right\rangle\right\rangle
consisting of all
symmetric bounded-degree power series
P\in\mathbf{k}\left\langle\left\langle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots\right\rangle\right\rangle
. This subset

\Lambda_{\operatorname*{NC}}
is a
\mathbf{k}
-subalgebra of
\mathbf{k}\left\langle\left\langle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots\right\rangle\right\rangle
, and is called the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra of symmetric functions in
noncommutative indeterminates
over
\mathbf{k}
.

This
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
\Lambda_{\operatorname*{NC}}
is called
\Pi\left(\mathbf{x}\right)
in
[
RosSag04
]
, and should not be mistaken for the
algebra
\operatorname*{NSym}
of noncommutative symmetric functions (which is
studied, e.g., in
[
GriRei14
, §5.4]
).
24
24

24

The latter algebra

\operatorname*{NSym}
can too be viewed as a subalgebra of
\mathbf{k}\left\langle\left\langle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots\right\rangle\right\rangle
, but it does not consist of symmetric power series (despite its name).

We can now define noncommutative chromatic symmetric functions of ambigraphs:

Definition 6.18
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a finite
ambigraph. Let
\mathbf{t}=\left(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{N}\right)
be a
finite list of elements of
V
that contains each element of
V
at least once.

(a)
For every
\mathbb{N}_{+}
-coloring
f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
of
G
, we let
\mathbf{X}_{f,\mathbf{t}}
denote the noncommutative
monomial
X_{f\left(t_{1}\right)}X_{f\left(t_{2}\right)}\cdots X_{f\left(t_{N}\right)}
in the indeterminates
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots
.

(b)
We define a noncommutative power series
Y_{G,\mathbf{t}}\in\mathbf{k}\left\langle\left\langle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots\right\rangle\right\rangle
by

Y_{G,\mathbf{t}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{ is
a}\\
\text{proper }\mathbb{N}_{+}\text{-coloring of }G\end{subarray}}\mathbf{X}_{f,\mathbf{t}}.

(28)

This power series
Y_{G,\mathbf{t}}
is called the
noncommutative
chromatic symmetric function
of
\left(G,\mathbf{t}\right)
.

Remark 6.19
.

Why did we require the list
\mathbf{t}
to contain each element of
V
at
least once in Definition
6.18
?

Otherwise, there could be a vertex
v\in V
that does not appear in

\mathbf{t}
. In that case, the monomials
\mathbf{X}_{f,\mathbf{t}}
would be
independent of the color
f\left(v\right)
of this vertex, and thus we
would obtain the same monomial
\mathbf{X}_{f,\mathbf{t}}
for infinitely many
different proper
\mathbb{N}_{+}
-colorings
f
(since we could arbitrarily
change the color
f\left(v\right)
without affecting the monomial

\mathbf{X}_{f,\mathbf{t}}
). Hence, the sum on the right hand side of
(
28
) would contain equally many identical
monomials, and this would render
Y_{G,\mathbf{t}}
undefined.

The noncommutative chromatic symmetric function
Y_{G,\mathbf{t}}
is a lift
of the weighted chromatic symmetric function
X_{G,w}
introduced in
Definition
6.13

(b)
. This can be made precise as follows:

Remark 6.20
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a
finite ambigraph. Let
\mathbf{t}=\left(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{N}\right)

be a finite list of elements of
V
that contains each element of
V
at least
once. Let
w:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
be the weight function on
V
that is
defined by

\displaystyle w\left(v\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\text{number of times that }v\text{ appears in
the list }\mathbf{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\text{number of all }i\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,N\right\}\text{
such that }t_{i}=v\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }v\in V.

Let
\pi:\mathbf{k}\left\langle\left\langle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots\right\rangle\right\rangle\rightarrow\mathbf{k}\left[\left[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots\right]\right]
be the topological
\mathbf{k}
-algebra
homomorphism that sends the noncommuting indeterminates
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots
to the respective commuting indeterminates
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},\ldots
. Then:

(a)
For every
\mathbb{N}_{+}
-coloring
f:V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
of
G
, we have
\pi\left(\mathbf{X}_{f,\mathbf{t}}\right)=\mathbf{x}_{f,w}
. (See Definition
6.13

(a)
and
Definition
6.18

(a)
for the meanings of

\mathbf{x}_{f,w}
and
\mathbf{X}_{f,t}
.)

(b)
We have
\pi\left(Y_{G,\mathbf{t}}\right)=X_{G,w}
.

We omit the simple proofs of these claims.

Our goal is now to state noncommutative analogues of our main theorems for
ambigraphs (specifically, Theorems
6.14
and

6.15
and Corollaries
6.16

and
6.17
). To do so, we need a noncommutative analogue
of the power-sum symmetric functions
p_{\lambda}
. In the commutative case,
we defined
p_{\lambda}
as the product
\prod\limits_{i\geq 1}p_{\lambda_{i}}
(see
Definition
1.4
). The noncommutative case, however, will not be
such a product, so we need to define it differently. This will require some preparations.

We begin by recalling the notion of a set partition:

Definition 6.21
.

Let
X
be a set.

(a)
A
set partition
of
X
means a set
\mathbf{P}
of
disjoint nonempty subsets of
X
such that
\bigcup\limits_{S\in\mathbf{P}}S=X
.

(b)
If
\mathbf{P}
is a set partition of
X
, then the sets

S\in\mathbf{P}
are called the
blocks
of
\mathbf{P}
.

For example:

•

The set
\left\{\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{2,4,5\right\}\right\}
is a set partition of the set
\left\{1,2,3,4,5\right\}
,
since
\left\{1,3\right\}
and
\left\{2,4,5\right\}
are two disjoint
nonempty subsets of
\left\{1,2,3,4,5\right\}
whose union is
\left\{1,3\right\}\cup\left\{2,4,5\right\}=\left\{1,2,3,4,5\right\}
.

•

The set
\left\{\left\{1,4\right\},\ \left\{2,5\right\},\ \left\{3,6\right\}\right\}
is a set partition of the set
\left\{1,2,3,4,5,6\right\}
. The blocks of this set partition are
\left\{1,4\right\}
and
\left\{2,5\right\}
and
\left\{3,6\right\}
.

•

The set
\left\{\left\{1,2,3,4,5\right\}\right\}
is a set
partition of the set
\left\{1,2,3,4,5\right\}
. It has only one block,
namely
\left\{1,2,3,4,5\right\}
.

•

The set
\left\{\left\{1\right\},\ \left\{2\right\},\ \left\{3\right\},\ \left\{4\right\},\ \left\{5\right\}\right\}
is a set
partition of the set
\left\{1,2,3,4,5\right\}
. It has five blocks,
namely
\left\{1\right\},\ \left\{2\right\},\ \left\{3\right\},\ \left\{4\right\},\ \left\{5\right\}
.

There is a well-known relation (actually a one-to-one correspondence) between
the set partitions of a given set
X
and the equivalence relations on
X
. It
can be summarized in the following theorem:

Theorem 6.22
.

Let
X
be a set.

(a)
If
\sim
is an equivalence relation on
X
, then the set

X/\left(\sim\right):=\left\{\text{all }\sim\text{-equivalence
classes}\right\}

is a set partition of
X
.

(b)
If
\mathbf{P}
is a set partition of
X
, then we can define an
equivalence relation
\sim
on the set
X
as follows: For any two elements

a
and
b
of
X
, we shall have
a\sim b
if and only if the elements
a

and
b
belong to the same block of
\mathbf{P}
. This relation
\sim
will be
called
\sim_{\mathbf{P}}
.

(c)
The maps

\displaystyle\left\{\text{equivalence relations on }X\right\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left\{\text{set partitions of }X\right\},

\displaystyle\left(\sim\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto X/\left(\sim\right)

and

\displaystyle\left\{\text{set partitions of }X\right\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left\{\text{equivalence relations on }X\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\sim_{\mathbf{P}}\right)

are mutually inverse bijections.

Example 6.23
.

Let
X
be the set
\left\{1,2,3,4,5,6\right\}
.

(a)
If
\sim
is the equivalence relation on
X
given by

\left(a\sim b\right)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \left(a\equiv b\operatorname{mod}2\right),

then the corresponding set partition
X/\left(\sim\right)
of
X
is

\left\{\left\{1,3,5\right\},\ \left\{2,4,6\right\}\right\}
.

(b)
If
\mathbf{P}
is the set partition
\left\{\left\{1,2\right\},\ \left\{3,4,6\right\},\ \left\{5\right\}\right\}
of

X
, then the corresponding equivalence relation
\sim_{\mathbf{P}}
on
X
is
given by

1\sim_{\mathbf{P}}2,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 3\sim_{\mathbf{P}}4\sim_{\mathbf{P}}6

and no further relations (except, of course, for the ones that follow from the
relations just given by reflexivity, symmetry and transitivity).

We can now define the noncommutative analogues of the power-sum symmetric
functions
p_{\lambda}
. These are indexed not by integer partitions
\lambda

but by set partitions
\mathbf{P}
:

Definition 6.24
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathbf{P}
be a set partition of the set
\left\{1,2,\ldots,N\right\}
. Recall the relation
\sim_{\mathbf{P}}
defined in
Theorem
6.22

(b)
.

Then, we define a noncommutative power series
P_{\mathbf{P}}\in\mathbf{k}\left\langle\left\langle X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots\right\rangle\right\rangle
by

P_{\mathbf{P}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{N}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{N};\\
i_{a}=i_{b}\text{ whenever }a\sim_{\mathbf{P}}b\end{subarray}}X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{N}}.

Here, the condition “
i_{a}=i_{b}
whenever
a\sim_{\mathbf{P}}b
” under the summation sign is shorthand for
“
i_{a}=i_{b}
for any two elements
a,b\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,N\right\}
that satisfy
a\sim_{\mathbf{P}}b
”.

This power series
P_{\mathbf{P}}
is called the
power-sum symmetric
function in noncommutative variables
corresponding to the set partition

\mathbf{P}
. It is not hard to see that it belongs to
\Lambda_{\operatorname*{NC}}
.

Note that
P_{\mathbf{P}}
is called
p_{\mathbf{P}}
in
[
GebSag01
, §2]

and in
[
RosSag04
]
.

Example 6.25
.

(a)
If
\mathbf{P}
is the set partition
\left\{\left\{1,3\right\},\ \left\{2,4,5\right\}\right\}
of
\left\{1,2,3,4,5\right\}
, then

\displaystyle P_{\mathbf{P}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{5};\\
i_{a}=i_{b}\text{
whenever }a\sim_{\mathbf{P}}b\end{subarray}}X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}X_{i_{3}}X_{i_{4}}X_{i_{5}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{5};\\
i_{1}=i_{3}\text{ and }i_{2}=i_{4}=i_{5}\end{subarray}}X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}X_{i_{3}}X_{i_{4}}X_{i_{5}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(u,v\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{2}}X_{u}X_{v}X_{u}X_{v}X_{v}

(here, we have substituted
\left(u,v,u,v,v\right)
for the summation
index
\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5}\right)
, since the condition
“
i_{1}=i_{3}
and
i_{2}=i_{4}=i_{5}
” is
saying precisely that the
5
-tuple
\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5}\right)
can be written in the form
\left(u,v,u,v,v\right)
).
This is a noncommutative power series that contains terms such as
X_{4}X_{7}X_{4}X_{7}X_{7}
or
X_{5}X_{5}X_{5}X_{5}X_{5}
(we are allowed to have

u=v
in the above sum), but not terms such as
X_{1}X_{2}X_{2}X_{1}X_{1}

(since the indeterminates don’t commute).

(b)
If
\mathbf{P}
is the set partition
\left\{\left\{1,4\right\},\ \left\{2,5\right\},\ \left\{3,6\right\}\right\}
of

\left\{1,2,3,4,5,6\right\}
, then

\displaystyle P_{\mathbf{P}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5},i_{6}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{6};\\
i_{a}=i_{b}\text{ whenever }a\sim_{\mathbf{P}}b\end{subarray}}X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}X_{i_{3}}X_{i_{4}}X_{i_{5}}X_{i_{6}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5},i_{6}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{6};\\
i_{1}=i_{4}\text{ and }i_{2}=i_{5}\text{ and }i_{3}=i_{6}\end{subarray}}X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}X_{i_{3}}X_{i_{4}}X_{i_{5}}X_{i_{6}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\left(u,v,w\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{3}}\underbrace{X_{u}X_{v}X_{w}X_{u}X_{v}X_{w}}_{=\left(X_{u}X_{v}X_{w}\right)^{2}}=\sum\limits_{\left(u,v,w\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{3}}\left(X_{u}X_{v}X_{w}\right)^{2}.

Note that this cannot be simplified to
\left(\sum\limits_{u\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}X_{u}^{2}\right)^{3}
, since the indeterminates don’t commute.

(c)
If
\mathbf{P}
is the set partition
\left\{\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}\right\}
of
\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
, then

\displaystyle P_{\mathbf{P}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{4};\\
i_{a}=i_{b}\text{ whenever }a\sim_{\mathbf{P}}b\end{subarray}}X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}X_{i_{3}}X_{i_{4}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{4};\\
i_{1}=i_{2}=i_{3}=i_{4}\end{subarray}}X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}X_{i_{3}}X_{i_{4}}=\sum\limits_{u\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}X_{u}X_{u}X_{u}X_{u}=\sum\limits_{u\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}X_{u}^{4}.

(d)
If
\mathbf{P}
is the set partition
\varnothing
of
\left\{{}\right\}
(so we have
N=0
), then

\displaystyle P_{\mathbf{P}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left({}\right)\in\left(\mathbb{N}_{+}\right)^{0};\\
i_{a}=i_{b}\text{ whenever }a\sim_{\mathbf{P}}b\end{subarray}}\left(\text{empty product}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\text{empty product}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since there is only one }0\text{-tuple}\right)

\displaystyle=1.

We shall now assign an equivalence relation to any finite graph
\left(V,E\right)
and any list
\mathbf{t}
of its vertices:

Proposition 6.26
.

Let
\left(V,B\right)
be a finite graph. Then,
according to Definition
1.7

(a)
, an equivalence
relation
\sim_{\left(V,B\right)}
is defined on the set
V
.

Let
\mathbf{t}=\left(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{N}\right)
be a finite list of
elements of
V
. Let
\approx
be the relation on the set
\left\{1,2,\ldots,N\right\}
defined as follows: Two elements
i
and
j
of

\left\{1,2,\ldots,N\right\}
shall satisfy
i\approx j
if and only if

t_{i}\sim_{\left(V,B\right)}t_{j}
.

Then, this relation
\approx
is an equivalence relation.

Proof 6.27
.

Straightforward and easy.

As we know from Theorem
6.22
, an equivalence relation is
“essentially the same as” a set partition.
Thus, in particular, we can turn the equivalence relation defined in
Proposition
6.26
into a set partition:

Definition 6.28
.

Let
\left(V,B\right)
be a finite graph. Let

\mathbf{t}=\left(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{N}\right)
be a finite list of
elements of
V
.

(a)
Let
\approx_{\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)}
be the relation

\approx
on the set
\left\{1,2,\ldots,N\right\}
defined in Proposition

6.26
. As we know from Proposition
6.26
,
this relation
\approx
is an equivalence relation. In other words, the
relation
\approx_{\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)}
is an equivalence relation.

(b)
Therefore, Theorem
6.22

(a)
(applied
to
X=\left\{1,2,\ldots,N\right\}
and
\left(\sim\right)=\left(\approx_{\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)}\right)
) shows that the set

\left\{1,2,\ldots,N\right\}/\left(\approx_{\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)}\right)=\left\{\text{all }\approx_{\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)}\text{-equivalence classes}\right\}

is a set partition of
\left\{1,2,\ldots,N\right\}
. We shall denote this
set partition by
\mathbf{P}\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)
.

Example 6.29
.

Let
\left(V,B\right)
be the finite graph with
V=\left\{u,v,w,x,y\right\}
and
B=\left\{\left\{u,v\right\},\ \left\{v,w\right\},\ \left\{x,y\right\}\right\}
. Then, the equivalence
relation
\sim_{\left(V,B\right)}
from Definition
1.7

(a)
satisfies
u\sim_{\left(V,B\right)}v\sim_{\left(V,B\right)}w
and
x\sim_{\left(V,B\right)}y
.

Let
\mathbf{t}=\left(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{N}\right)
be the list

\left(u,v,x,y,x,u\right)
of elements of
V
(so that
N=6
and
t_{1}=u

and
t_{2}=v
and
t_{3}=x
and
t_{4}=y
and
t_{5}=x
and
t_{6}=u
). Then,
the equivalence relation
\approx_{\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)}
from
Definition
6.28

(a)
satisfies

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\approx_{\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)}2\approx_{\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)}6\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }t_{1}\sim_{\left(V,B\right)}t_{2}\sim_{\left(V,B\right)}t_{6}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}

\displaystyle 3

\displaystyle\approx_{\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)}4\approx_{\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)}5\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }t_{3}\sim_{\left(V,B\right)}t_{4}\sim_{\left(V,B\right)}t_{5}\right)

and no further relations (except for the ones that follow from the relations
just given using reflexivity, symmetry and transitivity). Thus, the set
partition
\mathbf{P}\left(V,B,\mathbf{t}\right)
from Definition

6.28

(b)
is

\left\{\left\{1,2,6\right\},\ \left\{3,4,5\right\}\right\}.

We now have all notations in place to state noncommutative analogues of
Theorems
6.14
and
6.15
and
Corollaries
6.16
and
6.17
:

Theorem 6.30
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a
finite ambigraph. Let
\mathbf{t}=\left(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{N}\right)

be a finite list of elements of
V
that contains each element of
V
at least
once. Then,

Y_{G,\mathbf{t}}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}P_{\mathbf{P}\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F,\mathbf{t}\right)}.

Theorem 6.31
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a
finite ambigraph. Let
\mathbf{t}=\left(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{N}\right)

be a finite list of elements of
V
that contains each element of
V
at least
once. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a
labeling function. Let
\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of
G

(not necessarily containing all of them). Let
a_{K}
be an element of

\mathbf{k}
for every
K\in\mathfrak{K}
. Then,

Y_{G,\mathbf{t}}=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq F\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)P_{\mathbf{P}\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F,\mathbf{t}\right)}.

(Here, of course, the pair
\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right)
is
regarded as a graph, and the expression
\lambda\left(\left(V,\operatorname*{union}F\right),w\right)
is understood according to
Definition
6.5
.)

Corollary 6.32
.

Let
G=\left(V,E,\varphi\right)
be a
finite ambigraph. Let
\mathbf{t}=\left(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{N}\right)

be a finite list of elements of
V
that contains each element of
V
at least
once. Let
X
be a totally ordered set. Let
\ell:E\rightarrow X
be a
labeling function. Let
\mathfrak{K}
be some set of broken circuits of
G
