9
).

.

Now, we claim that, for every
B\in\mathcal{U}
and every
K\in\mathfrak{K}
,
we have the following logical equivalence:

\left(K\subseteq B\right)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \left(K\subseteq\Phi\left(B\right)\right).

(48)

Proof of (
48
):
Let

B\in\mathcal{U}
and
K\in\mathfrak{K}
. We must prove the equivalence
(
48
). The definition of
\Phi

yields
\Phi\left(B\right)=B\cup\left\{d\right\}\supseteq B
, so that

B\subseteq\Phi\left(B\right)
. Hence, if
K\subseteq B
, then
K\subseteq B\subseteq\Phi\left(B\right)
. Therefore, the forward implication of the
equivalence (
48
) is proven. It
thus remains to prove the backward implication of this equivalence. In other
words, it remains to prove that if
K\subseteq\Phi\left(B\right)
, then

K\subseteq B
. So let us assume that
K\subseteq\Phi\left(B\right)
.

We want to prove that
K\subseteq B
. Assume the contrary. Thus,

K\not\subseteq B
. We have
K\in\mathfrak{K}
. Thus,
K
is a broken circuit
of
M
(since
\mathfrak{K}
is a set of broken circuits of
M
). In other
words,
K
is a subset of
E
having the form
C\setminus\left\{e\right\}
, where
C
is a circuit of
M
, and where
e
is the unique element of
C

having maximum label (among the elements of
C
) (because this is how a broken
circuit is defined). Consider these
C
and
e
. Thus,
K=C\setminus\left\{e\right\}
.

The element
e
is the unique element of
C
having maximum label (among the
elements of
C
). Thus, if
e^{\prime}
is any element of
C
satisfying

\ell\left(e^{\prime}\right)\geq\ell\left(e\right)
, then

e^{\prime}=e.

(49)

But
\underbrace{K}_{\subseteq\Phi\left(B\right)=B\cup\left\{d\right\}}\setminus\left\{d\right\}\subseteq\left(B\cup\left\{d\right\}\right)\setminus\left\{d\right\}\subseteq B
.

If we had
d\notin K
, then we would have
K\setminus\left\{d\right\}=K

and therefore
K=K\setminus\left\{d\right\}\subseteq B
; this would
contradict
K\not\subseteq B
. Hence, we cannot have
d\notin K
. We thus
must have
d\in K
. Hence,
d\in K=C\setminus\left\{e\right\}
. Hence,

d\in C
and
d\neq e
.

But
C\setminus\left\{e\right\}=K\subseteq\Phi\left(B\right)\subseteq F
(since
\Phi\left(B\right)\in\mathcal{P}\left(F\right)
). On the
other hand, Statement
\mathfrak{F}_{1}
(of Lemma
8.12
) holds for
T=F
(since
F
is a flat of
M
). Hence, Statement

\mathfrak{F}_{2}
(of Lemma
8.12
) also holds for
T=F

(since Lemma
8.12
shows that these two statements are
equivalent). Thus, from
C\setminus\left\{e\right\}\subseteq F
, we obtain

C\subseteq F
. Thus,
e\in C\subseteq F
. Consequently,
\ell\left(d\right)\geq\ell\left(e\right)
(since
d
was defined to be an element
of
F
with maximum
\ell\left(d\right)
among all
d\in F
).

Also,
d\in C
. Since
\ell\left(d\right)\geq\ell\left(e\right)
, we
can therefore apply (
49
) to

e^{\prime}=d
. We thus obtain
d=e
. This contradicts
d\neq e
. This
contradiction proves that our assumption was wrong. Hence,
K\subseteq B
is
proven. Thus, we have proven the backward implication of the equivalence
(
48
); this completes the proof of
(
48
).

Now, proceeding as in the proof of (
12
), we
can show that

\sum\limits_{B\subseteq F}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}=\left[F=\varnothing\right].

This proves Lemma
8.32
.

We shall furthermore use a classical and fundamental result on the Möbius
function of any finite poset:

Proposition 8.34
.

Let
P
be a finite poset. Let
\mu
denote the
Möbius function of
P
.

(a)
For any
x\in P
and
y\in P
, we have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(x,z\right)=\left[x=y\right].

(50)

(b)
For any
x\in P
and
y\in P
, we have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,y\right)=\left[x=y\right].

(51)

(c)
Let
\mathbf{k}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-module. Let
\left(\beta_{x}\right)_{x\in P}
be a family of elements of
\mathbf{k}
. Then, every

z\in P
satisfies

\beta_{z}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in P;\\
y\leq z\end{subarray}}\mu\left(y,z\right)\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x\leq y\end{subarray}}\beta_{x}.

For the sake of completeness, let us give a self-contained proof of this
proposition (slicker arguments appear in the literature
40
40

40

For example,
Proposition
8.34

(c)
is equivalent to the

\Longrightarrow
implication of
[
Martin22
, (2.3a)]
.
):

Proof 8.35

(Proof of Proposition
8.34
.)
.

(a)
Let
x\in P

and
y\in P
. We must prove the equality (
50
). We
are in one of the following three cases:

Case 1:
We have
x=y
.

Case 2:
We have
x<y
.

Case 3:
We have neither
x=y
nor
x<y
.

Let us first consider Case 1. In this case, we have
x=y
. Hence, the sum

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(x,z\right)
contains
only one addend – namely, the addend for
z=x
. Thus,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(x,z\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(x,x\right)=1\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of the
M\"{o}bius function}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[x=y\right]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }x=y\right).

Thus, (
50
) is proven in Case 1.

Let us now consider Case 2. In this case, we have
x<y
. Hence,
x\neq y
, so
that
\left[x=y\right]=0
. Now,
y
is an element of
P
satisfying
x\leq y\leq y
. Thus, the sum
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(x,z\right)
contains an addend for
z=y
. Splitting off this addend, we
obtain

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(x,z\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y;\ z\neq y\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z<y\end{subarray}}}\mu\left(x,z\right)+\underbrace{\mu\left(x,y\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z<y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(x,z\right)\\
\text{(by (\ref{eq.def.moebius.rec2}))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z<y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(x,z\right)+\left(-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z<y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(x,z\right)\right)=0=\left[x=y\right].

Hence, (
50
) is proven in Case 2.

Finally, let us consider Case 3. In this case, we have neither
x=y
nor

x<y
. Thus, we do not have
x\leq y
. Hence, there exists no
z\in P

satisfying
x\leq z\leq y
. Thus,

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(x,z\right)=\left(\text{empty sum}\right)=0=\left[x=y\right]

(since we do not have
x=y
). Thus, (
50
) is
proven in Case 3.

Hence, (
50
) is proven in all three cases. This
proves Proposition
8.34

(a)
.

(b)
For any two elements
u
and
v
of
P
, we define a subset

\left[u,v\right]
of
P
by

\left[u,v\right]=\left\{w\in P\ \mid\ u\leq w\leq v\right\}.

This subset
\left[u,v\right]
is finite (since
P
is finite), and thus
its size
\left|\left[u,v\right]\right|
is a nonnegative integer.

We shall now prove Proposition
8.34

(b)
by
strong induction on
\left|\left[x,y\right]\right|
:

Induction step:
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that Proposition

8.34

(b)
holds whenever
\left|\left[x,y\right]\right|<N
. We must now prove that Proposition

8.34

(b)
holds whenever
\left|\left[x,y\right]\right|=N
.

We have assumed that Proposition
8.34

(b)
holds
whenever
\left|\left[x,y\right]\right|<N
. In other words, we
have assumed the following claim:

Claim 1:
For any
x\in P
and
y\in P
satisfying
\left|\left[x,y\right]\right|<N
, we have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,y\right)=\left[x=y\right].

Now, let
x
and
y
be two elements of
P
satisfying
\left|\left[x,y\right]\right|=N
. We are going to prove that

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,y\right)=\left[x=y\right].

(52)

We are in one of the following three cases:

Case 1:
We have
x=y
.

Case 2:
We have
x<y
.

Case 3:
We have neither
x=y
nor
x<y
.

In Case 1 and in Case 3, we can prove
(
52
) in exactly the same way as (in
our above proof of Proposition
8.34

(a)
) we
have proven (
50
). Thus, it remains only to prove
(
52
) in Case 2. In other words, we can
WLOG assume that we are in Case 2.

Assume this. Hence,
x<y
, so that
\left[x=y\right]=0
.

For every
t\in P
satisfying
x\leq t<y
, we have

\left|\left[x,t\right]\right|<N

(53)

41
41

41

Proof of (
53
):

Let
t\in P
be such that
x\leq t<y
. We shall proceed in several steps:

•

We have

\displaystyle\left[x,t\right]

\displaystyle=\left\{w\in P\ \mid\ x\leq w\leq t\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of }\left[x,t\right]\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{w\in P\ \mid\ x\leq w\leq y\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{because every }w\in P\text{ satisfying }w\leq t\\
\text{must also satisfy }w\leq y\text{ (since }t<y\text{)}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[x,y\right]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by the definition of
}\left[x,y\right]\right).

•

We have
t<y
. Thus, we do not have
y\leq t
. Hence, we do not have

x\leq y\leq t
. Hence,
y\notin\left[x,t\right]
. But
y\in\left[x,y\right]
(since
x\leq y\leq y
). Hence, the sets
\left[x,t\right]

and
\left[x,y\right]
are distinct (since the latter contains
y
but the
former does not). Combining this with
\left[x,t\right]\subseteq\left[x,y\right]
, we conclude that
\left[x,t\right]
is a proper subset of

\left[x,y\right]
. Hence,
\left|\left[x,t\right]\right|<\left|\left[x,y\right]\right|=N
. This proves
(
53
).

. Therefore, for every
t\in P
satisfying
x\leq t<y
, we have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq t\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,t\right)=\left[x=t\right]

(54)

(by Claim 1, applied to
t
instead of
y
). Also, for every
u\in P
and

v\in P
, we have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
u\leq t\leq v\end{subarray}}\mu\left(u,t\right)=\left[u=v\right]

(55)

42
42

42

Proof of (
55
):

Let
u\in P
and
v\in P
. Proposition
8.34

(a)

(applied to
x=u
and
y=v
) shows that
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
u\leq z\leq v\end{subarray}}\mu\left(u,z\right)=\left[u=v\right]
. Now,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
u\leq t\leq v\end{subarray}}\mu\left(u,t\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
u\leq z\leq v\end{subarray}}\mu\left(u,z\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have substituted }z\text{ for
}t\text{ in the sum}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[u=v\right].

This proves (
55
).

.

Now,

\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(z,t\right)\in P^{2};\\
x\leq z\leq t\leq y\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
z\leq t\leq y\end{subarray}}}\mu\left(z,t\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
z\leq t\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,t\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[z=y\right]\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.moebius.double0.short.b.smaller3})}\\
\text{(applied to
}u=z\text{ and }v=y\text{))}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\left[z=y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\text{ and }z=y\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left[z=y\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since }z=y\text{)}\end{subarray}}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\text{ and }z\neq y\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left[z=y\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since }z\neq y\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since every }z\in P\text{ satisfies either }z=y\text{ or }z\neq y\text{ (but not both)}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\text{ and }z=y\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{z\in\left\{w\in P\ \mid\ x\leq w\leq y\text{ and }w=y\right\}}}1+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\text{ and }z\neq y\end{subarray}}0}_{=0}=\sum\limits_{z\in\left\{w\in P\ \mid\ x\leq w\leq y\text{ and
}w=y\right\}}1

\displaystyle=\left|\underbrace{\left\{w\in P\ \mid\ x\leq w\leq y\text{ and
}w=y\right\}}_{=\left\{y\right\}}\right|=\left|\left\{y\right\}\right|=1.

Hence,

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(z,t\right)\in P^{2};\\
x\leq z\leq t\leq y\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
x\leq t\leq y\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq t\end{subarray}}}\mu\left(z,t\right)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
x\leq t\leq y\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq t\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,t\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
x\leq t\leq y\text{ and }t=y\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{t\in\left\{w\in P\ \mid\ x\leq w\leq y\text{ and
}w=y\right\}}=\sum\limits_{t\in\left\{y\right\}}\\
\text{(since }\left\{w\in P\ \mid\ x\leq w\leq y\text{ and }w=y\right\}=\left\{y\right\}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq t\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,t\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
x\leq t\leq y\text{ and
}t\neq y\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq t\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,t\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[x=t\right]\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.prop.moebius.double0.short.b.smaller2})}\\
\text{(since }t<y\text{
(because }t\leq y\text{ and }t\neq y\text{) and }x\leq t\text{))}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since every }t\in P\text{ satisfies either }t=y\text{ or }t\neq y\text{ (but not both)}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{t\in\left\{y\right\}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq t\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,t\right)}_{=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,y\right)}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
x\leq t\leq y\text{
and }t\neq y\end{subarray}}\left[x=t\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,y\right)+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
x\leq t\leq y\text{ and }t\neq y\end{subarray}}\left[x=t\right].

Subtracting
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,y\right)

from both sides of this equality, we obtain

\displaystyle 1-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,y\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
x\leq t\leq y\text{ and }t\neq y\end{subarray}}\left[x=t\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
x\leq t\leq y\text{ and }t=x\text{
and }t\neq y\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{t\in\left\{z\in P\ \mid\ x\leq z\leq y\text{ and }z=x\text{ and }z\neq y\right\}}=\sum\limits_{t\in\left\{x\right\}}\\
\text{(since }\left\{z\in P\ \mid\ x\leq z\leq y\text{ and }z=x\text{ and
}z\neq y\right\}=\left\{x\right\}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left[x=t\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since }x=t\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
x\leq t\leq y\text{ and
}t\neq x\text{ and }t\neq y\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left[x=t\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since }x\neq t\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since every }t\in P\text{ satisfies either }t=x\text{ or }t\neq x\text{ (but not both)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{t\in\left\{x\right\}}1+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in P;\\
x\leq t\leq y\text{ and }t\neq x\text{ and }t\neq y\end{subarray}}0}_{=0}=\sum\limits_{t\in\left\{x\right\}}1=1.

Solving this equality for
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,y\right)
, we obtain

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,y\right)=1-1=0=\left[x=y\right]

(since
x<y
). Thus, (
52
) is proven.

Let us now forget that we fixed
x
and
y
. We thus have proven that for any

x\in P
and
y\in P
satisfying
\left|\left[x,y\right]\right|=N
, we have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq y\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,y\right)=\left[x=y\right].

In other words, Proposition
8.34

(b)
holds
whenever
\left|\left[x,y\right]\right|=N
. This completes the
induction step. Thus, Proposition
8.34

(b)
is
proven by induction.

(c)
For every
v\in P
, we have

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in P;\\
y\leq v\end{subarray}}\mu\left(y,v\right)\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x\leq y\end{subarray}}\beta_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
z\leq v\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,v\right)\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x\leq z\end{subarray}}\beta_{x}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have renamed the summation}\\
\text{index }y\text{ as }z\text{ in the outer sum}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
z\leq v\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x\leq z\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{x\in P}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq v\end{subarray}}}\mu\left(z,v\right)\beta_{x}=\sum\limits_{x\in P}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in P;\\
x\leq z\leq v\end{subarray}}\mu\left(z,v\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[x=v\right]\\
\text{(by Proposition
\ref{prop.moebius.double0} {(b)}}\\
\text{(applied to }y=v\text{))}\end{subarray}}\beta_{x}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{x\in P}\left[x=v\right]\beta_{x}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x=v\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left[x=v\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since
}x=v\text{)}\end{subarray}}\beta_{x}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x\neq v\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left[x=v\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since }x\neq v\text{)}\end{subarray}}\beta_{x}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since every }x\in P\text{ satisfies either }x=v\text{ or }x\neq v\text{ (but not both)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x=v\end{subarray}}\beta_{x}+\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x\neq v\end{subarray}}0\beta_{x}}_{=0}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in P;\\
x=v\end{subarray}}\beta_{x}=\beta_{v}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since }v\in P\right).

Renaming
v
as
z
in this result, we obtain precisely Proposition

8.34

(c)
.

Proof 8.36

(Proof of Theorem
8.27
.)
.

If
T
is a subset of
E
,
then
\overline{T}
is a flat of
M
(by Proposition

8.17

(a)
). In other words, if
T
is a
subset of
E
, then
\overline{T}\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
. Renaming
T
as

B
in this statement, we conclude that if
B
is a subset of
E
, then

\overline{B}\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
.

For every
F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
, define an element
\beta_{F}\in\mathbf{k}
by

\beta_{F}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
\overline{B}=F\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right).

Now, using Lemma
8.32
, we can easily see that

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
G\subseteq F\end{subarray}}\beta_{G}=\left[F=\varnothing\right]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for every }F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M

(56)

43
43

43

Proof of (
56
):
Let

F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
. Thus,
F
is a flat of
M
.
If
B
is a subset of
E
, then the statements
\left(\overline{B}\subseteq F\right)
and
\left(B\subseteq F\right)
are equivalent. (This follows
from Proposition
8.17

(g)
, applied to

T=B
and
G=F
.)
Now,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
G\subseteq F\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\beta_{G}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
\overline{B}=G\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)\\
\text{(by the
definition of }\beta_{G}\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
G\subseteq F\end{subarray}}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
\overline{B}=G\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
\overline{B}\subseteq F\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(because if
}B\text{ is a subset of }E\text{,}\\
\text{then }\overline{B}\in\operatorname*{Flats}M\text{)}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
\overline{B}\subseteq F\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
B\subseteq F\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(because
if }B\text{ is a subset of }E\text{, then}\\
\text{the statements }\left(\overline{B}\subseteq F\right)\text{ and }\left(B\subseteq F\right)\text{ are}\\
\text{equivalent)}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
B\subseteq F\end{subarray}}}_{=\sum\limits_{B\subseteq F}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{B\subseteq F}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)=\left[F=\varnothing\right]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{eq.lem.matroid.NBCm.moeb.1})}\right).

This proves (
56
).

.

Let
\mu
be the Möbius function of the lattice
\operatorname*{Flats}M
.
The element
\overline{\varnothing}
is the global minimum of the poset

\operatorname*{Flats}M
.
44
44

44

This was proven during our proof of
Proposition
8.20
.
In particular,
\overline{\varnothing}\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
and
\overline{\varnothing}\subseteq F
. Hence,
\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)
is well-defined.

Now, fix
F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
. Proposition
8.34

(c)
(applied to
P=\operatorname*{Flats}M
and
z=F
) shows that

\displaystyle\beta_{F}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
y\subseteq F\end{subarray}}\mu\left(y,F\right)\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
x\subseteq y\end{subarray}}\beta_{x}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{since the relation
}\leq\text{ of the poset }\operatorname*{Flats}M\text{ is }\subseteq\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H\subseteq F\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
G\subseteq H\end{subarray}}\beta_{G}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left[H=\varnothing\right]\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.thm.matroid.charpol.varis.b-via-a}), applied to}\\
H\text{
instead of }F\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we renamed the
summation indices }y\text{ and }x\text{ as }H\text{ and }G\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H\subseteq F\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)\left[H=\varnothing\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H\subseteq F;\\
H=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)\underbrace{\left[H=\varnothing\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since }H=\varnothing\text{)}\end{subarray}}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H\subseteq F;\\
H\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)\underbrace{\left[H=\varnothing\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since }H\neq\varnothing\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H\subseteq F;\\
H=\varnothing\end{subarray}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(since the condition }H\subseteq F\\
\text{is
automatically implied by}\\
\text{the condition }H=\varnothing\text{)}\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right).

(57)

Now, we shall prove that

\beta_{F}=\left[\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\right]\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right).

(58)

Proof of (
58
):
We are in one of the
following two cases:

Case 1:
We have
\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing
.

Case 2:
We have
\overline{\varnothing}\neq\varnothing
.

Let us consider Case 1 first. In this case, we have
\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing
. Hence,
\varnothing=\overline{\varnothing}\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
. Thus, the sum
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)
has exactly
one addend: namely, the addend for
H=\varnothing
. Thus,
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)=\mu\left(\underbrace{\varnothing}_{=\overline{\varnothing}},F\right)=\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)
. Thus,
(
57
) becomes
\beta_{F}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)=\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)
. Comparing this with
\underbrace{\left[\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=1\\
\text{(since
}\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\text{)}\end{subarray}}\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)=\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)
, we obtain

\beta_{F}=\left[\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\right]\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)
. Thus,
(
58
) is proven in Case 1.

Let us now consider Case 2. In this case, we have
\overline{\varnothing}\neq\varnothing
. Thus, there exists no
H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
such that

H=\varnothing

45
45

45

Proof.
Assume the contrary. Thus,
there exists some
H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
such that
H=\varnothing
. In
other words,
\varnothing\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
. Hence,
\varnothing
is a
flat of
M
. Proposition
8.17

(b)

(applied to
G=\varnothing
) thus shows that
\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing
. This contradicts
\overline{\varnothing}\neq\varnothing
. This
contradiction proves that our assumption was wrong, qed.
. Hence, the sum

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)
is empty. Thus,
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)=\left(\text{empty sum}\right)=0
, so that (
57
) becomes
\beta_{F}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}H\in\operatorname*{Flats}M;\\
H=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\mu\left(H,F\right)=0
. Comparing this with
\underbrace{\left[\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\right]}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\\
\text{(since }\overline{\varnothing}\neq\varnothing\text{)}\end{subarray}}\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)=0
, we obtain
\beta_{F}=\left[\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\right]\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)
. Thus, (
58
) is proven in Case 2.

Now, we have proven (
58
) in both possible
Cases 1 and 2. Thus, (
58
) always holds.

Now, let us forget that we fixed
F
. We thus have proven
(
58
) for each
F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M
.

Now,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq F\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)x^{m-r_{M}\left(F\right)}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{B\subseteq E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\sum\limits_{F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
\overline{B}=F\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(because if }B\text{ is a subset of }E\text{,}\\
\text{then
}\overline{B}\in\operatorname*{Flats}M\text{)}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)\underbrace{x^{m-r_{M}\left(B\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x^{m-r_{M}\left(\overline{B}\right)}\\
\text{(since
Proposition \ref{prop.matroid.closure.props} {(f)} (applied to
}T=B\text{)}\\
\text{shows that }r_{M}\left(B\right)=r_{M}\left(\overline{B}\right)\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have renamed
the summation index }F\text{ as }B\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M}\ \ \sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
\overline{B}=F\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)\underbrace{x^{m-r_{M}\left(\overline{B}\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x^{m-r_{M}\left(F\right)}\end{subarray}}\\
\text{(since
}\overline{B}=F\text{)}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M}\ \ \underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq E;\\
\overline{B}=F\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|B\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq B\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\beta_{F}=\left[\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\right]\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.thm.matroid.charpol.varis.5}))}\end{subarray}}x^{m-r_{M}\left(F\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M}\left[\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\right]\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)x^{m-r_{M}\left(F\right)}

\displaystyle=\left[\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\right]\sum\limits_{F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M}\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)x^{m-r_{M}\left(F\right)}.

(59)

But the definition of
\chi_{M}
yields
\chi_{M}=\sum\limits_{F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M}\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)x^{m-r_{M}\left(F\right)}
. The definition of
\widetilde{\chi}_{M}

yields

\displaystyle\widetilde{\chi}_{M}

\displaystyle=\left[\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\right]\underbrace{\chi_{M}}_{=\sum\limits_{F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M}\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)x^{m-r_{M}\left(F\right)}}=\left[\overline{\varnothing}=\varnothing\right]\sum\limits_{F\in\operatorname*{Flats}M}\mu\left(\overline{\varnothing},F\right)x^{m-r_{M}\left(F\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{F\subseteq E}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}\left(\prod\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\in\mathfrak{K};\\
K\subseteq F\end{subarray}}a_{K}\right)x^{m-r_{M}\left(F\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by
(\ref{pf.thm.matroid.charpol.varis.9})}\right).

This proves Theorem
8.27
.

in the same way as Corollary

was derived from Theorem

.

Proof 8.38

(Proof of Theorem

.)

Theorem

.

Proof 8.39

(Proof of Corollary
8.30
.)
.

Corollary

8.30
follows from Corollary

8.29
when
\mathfrak{K}
is set to be the set of

all
broken circuits of
M
.

Proof 8.40

(Proof of Corollary
8.31
.)
.

If
F
is a subset of

E
such that
F
contains no broken circuit of
M
as a subset, then

r_{M}\left(F\right)=\left|F\right|

(60)

46
46

46

Proof of (
60
):
Let
F

be a subset of
E
such that
F
contains no broken circuit of
M
as a
subset.
We shall show that
F\in\mathcal{I}
. Indeed, assume the contrary. Thus,

F\notin\mathcal{I}
, so that
F\in\mathcal{P}\left(E\right)\setminus\mathcal{I}
. Hence, there exists a circuit
C
of
M
such that
C\subseteq F
(according to Lemma
8.8
, applied to
Q=F
). Consider this

C
. The set
C
is a circuit, and thus nonempty (because the empty set is in

\mathcal{I}
). Let
e
be the unique element of
C
having maximum label.
(This is clearly well-defined, since the labeling function
\ell
is
injective.) Then,
C\setminus\left\{e\right\}
is a broken circuit of
M

(by the definition of a broken circuit). Thus,
F
contains a broken circuit
of
M
as a subset (since
C\setminus\left\{e\right\}\subseteq C\subseteq F
). This contradicts the fact that
F
contains no broken circuit of
M
as a
subset. This contradiction shows that our assumption was wrong. Hence,

F\in\mathcal{I}
is proven.
Thus, Lemma
8.6
(applied to
T=F
) shows that
r_{M}\left(F\right)=\left|F\right|
, qed.

. Now, Corollary

8.30
yields

\widetilde{\chi}_{M}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{ contains no
broken}\\
\text{circuit of }M\text{ as a subset}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}\underbrace{x^{m-r_{M}\left(F\right)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=x^{m-\left|F\right|}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.cor.matroid.charpol.NBCfor.1}))}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}F\subseteq E;\\
F\text{ contains no broken}\\
\text{circuit of }M\text{ as a subset}\end{subarray}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}x^{m-\left|F\right|}.

This proves Corollary
8.31
.

8.4
A vanishing alternating sum for
matroids

As an application of the above, we can prove an analogue of the alternating
sum identity of Dahlberg and van Willigenburg (Theorem
7.1

above) for the characteristic polynomials of matroids:

Theorem 8.41
.

Let
M=\left(E,\mathcal{I}\right)
be a matroid.
Let
m=r_{M}\left(E\right)
. Let
C
be a circuit of
M
, and let
e\in C

be arbitrary. Then,

\sum\limits_{F\subseteq C\setminus\left\{e\right\}}\left(-1\right)^{\left|F\right|}x^{m-r_{M}\left(E\setminus F\right)}\cdot\widetilde{\chi}_{M\setminus F}=0.

Here, whenever
F
is a subset of
E
, the notation
M\setminus F
denotes the
matroid
\left(E\setminus F,\ \mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{P}\left(E\setminus F\right)\right)
(that is, the matroid whose ground set is
E\setminus F

and whose independent sets are those subsets of
E\setminus F
that are
independent in
M
).

Proof 8.42

(Proof of Theorem
8.41
.)
.

Quite similar to our above proof
of Theorem
7.2
, but using Theorem

8.28
and Corollary
8.29

instead of Theorem
6.37
and Corollary

6.39
. (We also need to observe that the rank
function
r_{M\setminus F}
is a restriction of
r_{M}
whenever
F
is a
subset of
E
.) We leave all details to the reader.
